Q1Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterMechanical Properties of Solids high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the elastic potential energy stored in a stretched wire, U = (1/2) x load x extension = (1/2)Mgl, which is taught in Mechanical Properties of Solids.
subtopic 1Elastic Potential Energy of a Strained Body
why this subtopicThe answer is the elastic potential energy stored in a stretched wire, U = (1/2) x load x extension = (1/2)Mgl.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
When a block of mass M is suspended by a long wire of lenght L, the length of the wire becomes (L + l). The elastic potential energy stored in the extended wire is:
  1. Mgl
  2. MgL
  3. 12Mgl
  4. 12MgL
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. The stretching force on the wire is F = Mg and the extension is Δ L = (L + l) - L = l.
  2. Elastic potential energy stored in a stretched wire is U = 12 F   Δ L.
  3. U = 12(Mg)(l) = 12Mgl, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultyeasy · 1 step
stepsrecall the NCERT result that elastic energy stored in a stretched wire is 12 x load x extension and substitute load = Mg, extension = l
whyA single substitution of F = Mg and extension l into the chapter's stated expression U = 12 F x for a stretched wire.
the catchOption Mgl is the work done by gravity (loss of gravitational PE), a trap for a candidate who equates it with the stored elastic energy; options with L test mistaking original length for extension.

Q2Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterLaws of Motion medium confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the string tension in vertical circular motion being greatest at the lowest point (T = mg + mv^2/r), a Newton's-second-law / tension-in-circular-motion result.
subtopic 1Tension in String in Circular Motion
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the tension in a string whirled in a vertical circle being greatest at the lowest point (T = mg + mv^2/r).
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A mass m is attached to a thin wire and whirled in a vertical circle. The wire is most likely to break when:
  1. the mass is at the highest point
  2. the wire is horizontal
  3. the mass is at the lowest point
  4. inclined at an angle of 60° from vertical
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Radial equation at angle θ from the lowest point: T - mgcosθ = mv2r.
  2. So T = mv2r + mgcosθ.
  3. Energy conservation gives v largest at the lowest point (θ = 0), where cosθ = 1 is also largest.
  4. Hence Tmax = mvlow2r + mg at the lowest point, so the wire is most likely to break there: option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultyeasy · 1 step
stepsRecall that in vertical circular motion the tension is T = mv2/r + mg cos(θ) measured from the lowest point, which is maximum at the lowest point where speed is greatest and gravity acts directly against tension
whyA single recall of where tension peaks in a vertical circle (lowest point, where both the required centripetal force and the weight component add to the tension) answers it with no calculation.

Q3Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterMagnetic Effect of Electric Current high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the radius of a charged particle moving in a uniform magnetic field, r = p/(qB), so with equal momenta r is proportional to 1/q, giving r_H : r_alpha = 2 : 1.
subtopic 1Motion in a Magnetic Field
why this subtopicThe answer uses the radius of circular motion of a charged particle in a uniform magnetic field, r = p/(qB), so with equal momenta r_H : r_alpha = q_alpha : q_H = 2 : 1.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Ionized hydrogen atoms and α-particles with same momenta enters perpendicular to a constant magnetic field, B. The ratio of their radii of their paths rH : rα will be :
  1. 2 : 1
  2. 1 : 2
  3. 4 : 1
  4. 1 : 4
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. For a charge moving perpendicular to B: qvB = mv2r, so r = mvqB = pqB.
  2. With the same p and B, r ∝ 1q.
  3. qH = e (proton) and qα = 2e.
  4. rHrα = qαqH = 2ee = 2, so rH : rα = 2 : 1, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRecall the radius of a charged particle moving perpendicular to a uniform field, r = mv/(qB) = p/(qB), so with p and B equal r is inversely proportional to q → Take the charges of the ionized hydrogen atom (e) and the α particle (2e) and form the inverse ratio r_H : r_α
whyThe work is recalling r = p/(qB) and applying it as an inverse proportion in charge, after identifying the charges of a proton and an α particle.

Q4Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterWork, Energy and Power high confidence
why this chapterThe answer 8/9 follows from conservation of momentum and kinetic energy in a head-on elastic collision (fraction of KE transferred = 4m1m2/(m1+m2)^2), which is taught under elastic collisions in Work, Energy and Power.
subtopic 1Problems on Elastic Collision
why this subtopicThe answer 8/9 comes from the head-on elastic collision velocity formula v_A = (m1-m2)u/(m1+m2) = u/3, so A keeps 1/9 of its kinetic energy and loses 8/9.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Body A of mass 4m moving with speed u collides with another body B of mass 2m, at rest. The collision is head on and elastic in nature. After the collision the fraction of energy lost by the colliding body A is :
  1. 19
  2. 89
  3. 49
  4. 59
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. For a 1-D elastic collision with B at rest, vA = m1 - m2m1 + m2 u.
  2. vA = 4m - 2m4m + 2m u = u3.
  3. KA'KA = (vAu)2 = 19.
  4. Fraction of energy lost by A = 1 - 19 = 89, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecall the 1-D elastic collision result for the incident body's final velocity, v_A = (m_A - m_B) u / (m_A + m_B), and substitute m_A = 4m, m_B = 2m → Form the ratio of A's final to initial kinetic energy, (v_A/u)2 → Subtract that retained fraction from 1 to get the fraction of energy lost by A
whyThe work is one recalled elastic-collision velocity formula with a mass substitution, followed by squaring a velocity ratio and taking its complement to convert retained energy into lost energy.
the catchOne option is exactly the fraction of kinetic energy A retains, so a candidate who stops after the KE ratio and skips the 'lost' complement lands on a printed distractor.

Q5Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterWave Optics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the angular width in a double-slit experiment being proportional to wavelength, which shrinks by the refractive index in water (0.2/(4/3) = 0.15 degrees). That is wave optics.
subtopic 1Fringe Width in YDSE
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the angular fringe width in a double-slit experiment being proportional to wavelength (theta = lambda/d), so immersing in water divides it by mu: 0.2/(4/3) = 0.15 degrees.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
In a double slit experiment, when light of wavelength 400 nm was used, the angular width of the first minima formed on a screen placed 1 m away, was found to be 0.2°. What will be the angular width of the first minima, if the entire experimental apparatus is immersed in water? (μwater = 4/3)
  1. 0.266°
  2. 0.15°
  3. 0.05°
  4. 0.1°
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. The angular width of a fringe is θ = λd, which does not depend on the screen distance D.
  2. In water the wavelength becomes λ' = λμ = 400 nm4/3 = 300 nm.
  3. So θ' = λ'd = θμ.
  4. θ' = 0.2° × 34 = 0.15°, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRecall that angular width in a double-slit pattern is proportional to wavelength (θ = λ/d), independent of screen distance → Apply that the wavelength in water is λ/μ, so scale the given 0.2 degrees by 1/μ = 3/4
whyA recall of the angular-width relation plus one application of the wavelength reduction in a medium, followed by a single scaling of the given angle.
the catchOne option is exactly the result of multiplying by mu instead of dividing, and the stated 1 m screen distance is irrelevant data that may tempt a linear-width calculation.

Q6Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterElectromagnetic Induction high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on knowing the applications of eddy currents (induction furnace, magnetic braking, electromagnet) versus Joule heating in an electric heater, which is taught under eddy currents in electromagnetic induction.
subtopic 1Eddy Currents
why this subtopicThe answer depends on knowing the applications of eddy currents (induction furnace, magnetic braking, electromagnet damping) and recognising that an electric heater works by Joule heating instead.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
In which of the following devices, the eddy current effect is not used?
  1. Induction furnace
  2. Magnetic braking in train
  3. Electromagnet
  4. Electric heater
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · medium
  1. NCERT lists the applications of eddy currents: magnetic braking in trains, electromagnetic damping, induction furnaces and electric power meters.
  2. An induction furnace heats metal through eddy currents set up by a high-frequency alternating field, so it uses the effect.
  3. Magnetic braking in trains uses eddy currents induced in the rails or discs to oppose the motion, so it uses the effect.
  4. An electric heater produces heat as H = I2 R t in its resistive element (Joule heating), with no eddy currents involved, so option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 mediumagree
difficultyeasy · 1 step
stepsRecall the NCERT list of eddy-current applications (magnetic braking in trains, electromagnetic damping, induction furnace, electric power meters) and pick the device not on it
whyA single recall of the applications of eddy currents listed in the NCERT section on eddy currents, matched against four named devices.
the catchMore than one option can be read as a device that does not rely on eddy currents, so the candidate has to judge which one the setter means rather than just spotting the only device off the list.

Q7Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterMechanical Properties of Fluids high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the excess pressure inside a soap bubble (4T/r) equated to the hydrostatic pressure rho*g*Z0, both taught in Mechanical Properties of Fluids.
subtopic 1Excess Pressure on Curved Surface (General Case, Drop, Soap Bubbles, Air Bubbles)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the excess pressure inside a soap bubble, 4T/r = 100 Pa, set equal to the gauge pressure rho*g*Z0 to give Z0 = 1 cm.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A soap bubble, having radius of 1 mm, is blown from a detergent solution having a surface tension of 2.5 × 10-2 N/m. The pressure inside the bubble equals at a point Z0 below the free surface of water in a container. Taking g = 10 m/s2, density of water = 103 kg/m3, the value of Z0 is :
  1. 100 cm
  2. 10 cm
  3. 1 cm
  4. 0.5 cm
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. A soap bubble has two free surfaces, so the excess pressure inside is Δ P = 4Tr.
  2. Δ P = 4 × 2.5 × 10-21 × 10-3 = 100 Pa.
  3. Pressure inside the bubble = P0 + Δ P, and pressure at depth Z0 in water = P0 + ρ g Z0.
  4. Equating: ρ g Z0 = Δ P ⇒ Z0 = 100103 × 10 = 0.01 m.
  5. Z0 = 0.01 m = 1 cm, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecall that a soap bubble has two free surfaces, so its excess pressure is 4T/r (not 2T/r), and evaluate it with r = 1 mm converted to metres → Read 'pressure inside the bubble equals pressure at depth Z0' as excess (gauge) pressure equalling the hydrostatic term ρ*g*Z0, with atmospheric pressure cancelling on both sides → Solve Z0 = (4T/r)/(ρ*g) and convert the result from metres to centimetres to match the options
whyThe work is a formula recall for the excess pressure of a two-surface bubble, then equating it to ρ*g*h and solving with SI-to-cm conversion.
the catchOne option is exactly the result of using the single-surface (liquid drop) formula 2T/r instead of 4T/r, and the stem leaves it unstated that atmospheric pressure cancels so only gauge pressures are compared.

Q9Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterRotational Motion high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the total kinetic energy of a rolling disc, translational plus rotational (3/4 mv^2 = 3 J), which is taught under rolling motion in Rotational Motion.
subtopic 1Rolling Motion
why this subtopicThe answer is the total kinetic energy of a rolling disc, (1/2)mv^2(1 + k^2/R^2) = (3/4)mv^2 = 3 J, which by the work-energy theorem is the work needed to stop it; this is taught under rolling motion.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A disc of radius 2 m and mass 100 kg rolls on a horizontal floor. Its centre of mass has speed of 20 cm/s. How much work is needed to stop it?
  1. 3 J
  2. 30 kJ
  3. 2 J
  4. 1 J
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Work needed to stop the disc equals its total kinetic energy (work-energy theorem).
  2. For rolling without slipping, KE = 12mv2 + 12Iω2 with I = 12mR2 and ω = vR.
  3. KE = 12mv2 + 14mv2 = 34mv2.
  4. v = 20 cm/s = 0.2 m/s, so KE = 34 × 100 × (0.2)2 = 3 J, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsConvert the centre-of-mass speed from 20 cm/s to 0.2 m/s and recognise by the work-energy theorem that the work needed equals the disc's total kinetic energy → Write total KE of a rolling disc as translational plus rotational, using I = 12MR2 and the rolling condition v = ω R, giving KE = 34Mv2 → Substitute M = 100 kg and v = 0.2 m/s to evaluate the energy
whyThe work is a recall of rolling kinetic energy (translational plus rotational with the disc's moment of inertia) applied through the work-energy theorem, followed by one substitution after a cm-to-m conversion.
the catchOptions include the exact results of plausible wrong routes: translational KE only, rotational KE only, and the value obtained by not converting cm/s to m/s.

Q10Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterOscillations high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on combining A sin(wt) + B cos(wt) into a single SHM of amplitude sqrt(A^2 + B^2), with the constant A0 only shifting the mean position; this is SHM amplitude, taught in Oscillations.
subtopic 1Calculate Amplitude of SHM
subtopic 2Composition of Two SHMs
why this subtopicThe answer is the amplitude sqrt(A^2+B^2), found by combining the two same-frequency SHMs A sin(wt) and B cos(wt) (90 degrees out of phase) while the constant A0 only shifts the mean position.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The displacement of a particle executing simple harmonic motion is given by y = A0 + Asinω t + Bcosω t Then the amplitude of its oscillation is given by :
  1. A0 + A2 + B2
  2. A2 + B2
  3. A02 + (A + B)2
  4. A + B
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Write y - A0 = Asinω t + Bcosω t; the constant A0 only shifts the mean position and does not change the amplitude.
  2. Set A = Rcosφ and B = Rsinφ, so Asinω t + Bcosω t = Rsin(ω t + φ).
  3. Squaring and adding: R2 = A2 + B2, so R = A2 + B2.
  4. Hence y = A0 + A2 + B2 sin(ω t + φ) and the amplitude is A2 + B2, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRecognise that the constant term A0 only shifts the mean (equilibrium) position and does not contribute to the amplitude → Combine A sin(wt) + B cos(wt), two same-frequency SHMs in phase quadrature, into a single R sin(wt + φ) and read off the resultant amplitude R from A and B
whyThe work is separating the constant offset from the oscillating part, then using the standard rule for adding two perpendicular-phase SHMs of equal frequency to get one amplitude.
the catchOption 1 is exactly what a candidate gets by wrongly adding the offset A_0 to the resultant amplitude, and option 4 is what they get by adding A and B as scalars without accounting for the phase difference.

Q11Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterRay Optics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the lens maker formula and the focal length of thin lenses combined in contact (the glycerin layer acting as a concave lens), which is ray optics.
subtopic 1Combination of Thin Lenses in Contact
subtopic 2Lens Maker Formula
why this subtopicThe answer rests on adding powers of lenses in contact (1/F = sum of 1/f), with the glycerin layer treated as a concave lens whose focal length (-f) comes from the lens maker formula, giving F1 = f/2 and F2 = f.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Two similar thin equi-convex lenses, of focal length f each, are kept coaxially in contact with each other such that the focal length of the combination is F1. When the space between the two lenses is filled with glycerin (which has the same refractive index (μ = 1.5) as that of glass) then the equivalent focal length is F2. The ratio F1 : F2 will be :
  1. 2 : 1
  2. 1 : 2
  3. 2 : 3
  4. 3 : 4
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. For each equi-convex glass lens, 1f = (μ - 1)(2R) = 0.5 × 2R = 1R, so R = f.
  2. Two lenses in contact: 1F1 = 1f + 1f = 2f, so F1 = f2.
  3. Glycerin filling the gap forms an equi-concave lens with radii R: 1fg = (1.5 - 1)(-2R) = -1f.
  4. Combination of three lenses: 1F2 = 2f - 1f = 1f, so F2 = f.
  5. F1 : F2 = f2 : f = 1 : 2, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsCombine two thin lenses in contact to get F1 = f/2 → Apply the lens-maker formula to the equi-convex glass lens (μ = 1.5) to express its radius of curvature in terms of f (R = f) → Recognise that the glycerin filling the gap forms an equi-concave lens with the same radii and use the lens-maker formula to get its focal length (-f) → Combine the three lenses in contact (two convex glass, one concave glycerin) to get F2, then form the ratio F1 : F2
whyThe work is two lens-maker calculations plus two combinations of lenses in contact, hinging on modelling the glycerin layer as a separate concave lens.
the catchThe non-obvious step is treating the glycerin between the lenses as a third, diverging lens; the 2 : 1 option is the inverted ratio, a ready trap for a candidate who flips F1 and F2.

Q12Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterKinetic Theory of Gases high confidence
why this chapterThe answer rests on the kinetic-theory result that the average kinetic energy of gas molecules is proportional to absolute temperature, so heating the gas increases its kinetic energy.
subtopic 1RMS Speed and Its Dependence on Temperature
why this subtopicThe answer rests on the kinetic interpretation of temperature: average kinetic energy (1/2 m v_rms^2 = 3/2 kT) rises with temperature, which is taught with rms speed and its temperature dependence.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Increase in temperature of a gas filled in a container would lead to :
  1. Increase in its mass
  2. Increase in its kinetic energy
  3. Decrease in its pressure
  4. Decrease in intermolecular distance
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Average kinetic energy per molecule is Ek = 32 kB T, so a rise in T raises the kinetic energy, which is option 2; mass stays the same, and in a fixed container P ∝ T rises while intermolecular distance does not decrease, so options 1, 3 and 4 are wrong.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyeasy · 1 step
stepsRecall from kinetic theory that the average kinetic energy of gas molecules is proportional to absolute temperature, and match it to the option for kinetic energy (the others on mass, falling pressure and intermolecular distance go against the same basic idea for a fixed container)
whyA single recall of the kinetic-theory result that molecular kinetic energy rises with temperature, matched straight to one option.

Q13Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterModern Physics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the de Broglie wavelength of an electron accelerated through a potential V (lambda = h/sqrt(2meV)), which is taught in Modern Physics (dual nature of matter).
subtopic 1de Broglie Wavelength of an Accelerated Charged Particle
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the de Broglie wavelength of an electron accelerated through a potential V, lambda = h/sqrt(2meV) = 1.227/sqrt(V) nm, giving about 12.2 x 10^-12 m for 10,000 V.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
An electron is accelerated through a potential difference of 10,000 V. Its de Broglie wavelength is, (nearly) : (me = 9 × 10-31 kg)
  1. 12.2 × 10-13 m
  2. 12.2 × 10-12 m
  3. 12.2 × 10-14 m
  4. 12.2 nm
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Kinetic energy gained: p22m = eV, so p = 2meV.
  2. λ = hp = h2meV.
  3. 2meV = 2 × 9×10-31 × 1.6×10-19 × 104 = 2.88×10-45 ≈ 5.37×10-23 kg m/s.
  4. λ = 6.63×10-345.37×10-23 ≈ 1.23×10-11 m (equivalently 1.227104 nm = 0.01227 nm).
  5. λ ≈ 12.2×10-12 m, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRecall the NCERT result for an electron accelerated through V volts, λ = 1.227/V nm (or derive it from λ = h/2meV), and substitute V = 10,000 V so V = 100, giving 0.01227 nm → Convert 0.01227 nm into metres in the 12.2 x 10^n m form the options use, to pick the correct power of ten
whyThe work is one substitution into a formula the chapter states, followed by a unit conversion from nm to m in which the power of ten decides the option.
the catchEvery option has the same mantissa 12.2 and differs only in the power of ten (or unit), so a slip in the nm-to-m or angstrom-to-m conversion lands on a distractor.

Q14Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterThermal Properties of Matter high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on linear thermal expansion (equal expansion requires L_Cu*alpha_Cu = L_Al*alpha_Al, giving 68 cm), which is taught under Thermal Expansion in Thermal Properties of Matter.
subtopic 1Thermal Expansion
why this subtopicThe answer uses linear thermal expansion: for equal length changes alpha_Cu*L_Cu = alpha_Al*L_Al, giving L_Al = 1.7*88/2.2 = 68 cm.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A copper rod of 88 cm and an aluminium rod of unknown length have their increase in length independent of increase in temperature. The length of aluminium rod is : (αCu = 1.7 × 10-5 K-1 and αAl = 2.2 × 10-5 K-1)
  1. 6.8 cm
  2. 113.9 cm
  3. 88 cm
  4. 68 cm
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. The difference in lengths stays the same at every temperature only if Δ LCu = Δ LAl.
  2. LCu αCu Δ T = LAl αAl Δ T ⇒ LAl = LCu αCuαAl.
  3. LAl = 88 × 1.7 × 10-52.2 × 10-5 = 149.62.2 = 68 cm, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps

chapterRotational Motion high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on torque = I*alpha with the moment of inertia of a solid cylinder (MR^2/2) and rotational kinematics (omega^2 = 2*alpha*theta), giving 2 x 10^-6 N m, which is rotational motion.
subtopic 1Problems on Torque of a Force About the Axis of Rotation
why this subtopicThe answer is the stopping torque found from tau = I*alpha, with alpha taken from the rotational kinematics relation omega^2 = 2*alpha*theta for a cylinder of I = MR^2/2, which gives 2 x 10^-6 N m.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A solid cylinder of mass 2 kg and radius 4 cm rotating about its axis at the rate of 3 rpm. The torque required to stop after 2π revolutions is
  1. 2 × 10-6 N m
  2. 2 × 10-3 N m
  3. 12 × 10-4 N m
  4. 2 × 106 N m
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. ω0 = 3 rpm = 3 × 2π60 = π10 rad s-1.
  2. θ = 2π rev = 2π × 2π = 4π2 rad.
  3. 0 = ω02 - 2αθ ⇒ α = ω022θ = π2/1008π2 = 1800 rad s-2.
  4. I = 12MR2 = 12 × 2 × (0.04)2 = 1.6 × 10-3 kg m2.
  5. τ = Iα = 1.6 × 10-3800 = 2 × 10-6 N m, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultyhard · 5 steps
stepscompute the moment of inertia of the solid cylinder about its axis, I = MR2/2, after converting the radius from cm to m → convert the angular speed from rpm to rad/s (ω = 2*π*3/60) → convert the angular displacement of 2*π revolutions into radians (θ = 2*π * 2*π) → use rotational kinematics ω2 = 2*α*θ to find the angular deceleration → multiply torque = I*α to get the required torque
whyThe work chains a moment-of-inertia calculation with two unit conversions (rpm to rad/s and revolutions to radians) and a rotational-kinematics equation before the torque relation can be applied.
the catchThree separate unit conversions (cm to m, rpm to rad/s, revolutions to radians) each shift the order of magnitude of the result, and the options are spread across powers of ten so a missed conversion lands on a different option.

  1. V2 > V1 and i1 = i2

A hollow metal sphere of radius R is uniformly charged. The electric field due to the sphere at a distance r from the centre
  1. Increases as r increases for r < R and for r > R
  2. Zero as r increases for r < R, decreases as r increases for r > R

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high

Q28Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterRay Optics high confidence

subtopic 1Total Internal Reflection (TIR)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the definition of the critical angle: at incidence equal to the critical angle the refracted ray grazes the interface, so the angle of refraction is 90 degrees, which is taught under total internal reflection.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
In total internal reflection when the angle of incidence is equal to the critical angle for the pair of media in contact, what will be angle of refraction?
  1. 180°
  2. 0°
  3. Equal to angle of incidence
  4. 90°
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Snell's law at the interface: n1 sin ic = n2 sin r, with sin ic = n2n1 by definition of the critical angle.
  2. Substituting: n1 · n2n1 = n2 sin r ⇒ sin r = 1.
  3. r = 90°, the refracted ray grazes the surface, which is option 4.

Q29Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterGravitation high confidence
why this chapterThe answer mgR/2 comes from the change in gravitational potential energy -GMm/r between r = R and r = 2R, which Gravitation teaches. The answer cannot come from mgh because g is not constant over that height.
subtopic 1Gravitational Potential Energy
why this subtopicThe answer comes from the change in gravitational potential energy, -GMm/2R - (-GMm/R) = GMm/2R = (1/2)mgR, when the mass is raised from r = R to r = 2R.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The work done to raise a mass m from the surface of the earth to a height h, which is equal to the radius of the earth, is:
  1. mgR
  2. 2 mgR
  3. 12mgR
  4. 32mgR
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Potential energy at distance r from the centre: U(r) = -GMmr.
  2. At the surface r = R: U1 = -GMmR; at height h = R, r = 2R: U2 = -GMm2R.
  3. W = U2 - U1 = -GMm2R + GMmR = GMm2R.
  4. Using GM = gR2: W = gR2 m2R = 12mgR, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree

stepsRecognise that mgh does not apply because h = R is not small compared with the earth's radius, so the change in gravitational potential energy -GMm/r must be used → Write the work as the potential energy difference between r = R and r = 2R, giving GMm/(2R) → Substitute GM = gR2 to express the result in terms of mgR
whyThe work is a recall of the gravitational potential energy formula applied between two radii, followed by the substitution GM = gR2 to convert into the options' form.

chapterLaws of Motion high confidence
why this chapterThe answer comes from static friction (mu*N) balancing the weight while the normal force from the wall supplies the centripetal force (N = m*omega^2*r), giving omega = sqrt(g/(mu*r)) = 10 rad/s, which is friction and circular dynamics under Laws of Motion.
subtopic 1Centripetal and Centrifugal Force
subtopic 2Static Friction
why this subtopicThe answer combines the wall's normal force supplying the centripetal force (N = m*omega^2*r) with limiting static friction balancing the weight (mu*N = mg), giving omega = sqrt(g/(mu*r)) = 10 rad/s.
printed in the booklet

A block of mass 10 kg is in contact against the inner wall of a hollow cylindrical drum of radius 1 m. The coefficient of friction between the block and the inner wall of the cylinder is 0.1.   2. 102π rad/s
  3. 10 rad/s
  4. 10 π rad/s

no worked solution is printed for this question

  1. The normal reaction of the wall provides the centripetal force: N = mω2 r.
  2. For the block to stay stationary, maximum friction must balance weight: μ N = mg.
  3. Substitute: μ m ω2 r = mg ⇒ ω2 = gμ r.
  4. ω2 = 100.1 × 1 = 100 ⇒ ωmin = 10 rad/s, which is option 3.

why this subtopicThe answer depends on the field of an infinite line charge, E = lambda/(2 pi epsilon_0 r), at r = R from each line, with the two fields adding in the same direction midway between them to give lambda/(pi epsilon_0 R).
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Two parallel infinite line charges with linear charge densities +λ C/m and -λ C/m are placed at a distance of 2R in free space. What is the electric field mid-way between the two line charges?

  2. 2λπε0 R N/C
  3. λπε0 R N/C
  4. λ2πε0 R N/C
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Field of an infinite line charge at distance r: E = λ2πε0 r.
  2. At the midpoint r = R for each line, so each gives E1 = E2 = λ2πε0 R.
  3. The field of +λ points away from it and the field of -λ points towards it, so at the midpoint both point the same way (from +λ to -λ).
  4. Enet = E1 + E2 = 2λ2πε0 R = λπε0 R N/C, which is option 3.

the catchDistractors match plausible wrong routes: 'Zero' is the result of assuming the fields cancel as for like charges, and the single-line-charge value is the result of forgetting to add the second field.

subtopic 1Logic Gates
why this subtopicThe answer is that a switch circuit where the LED goes off only when both switches A and B are closed realises the NAND Boolean operation, which is a logic gate identification.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The correct Boolean operation represented by the circuit diagram drawn is :
  1. AND
  2. OR
  3. NAND
  4. NOR
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Switches A and B are in series, joining the node between the top R and the LED to ground.
  2. If either switch is open (A=0 or B=0), no path to ground exists, so current flows through the LED: Y=1.
  3. If both are closed (A=B=1), the node is shorted to ground and the LED goes off: Y=0.
  4. Truth table Y = 1,1,1,0 gives Y = A · B, which is NAND, option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict

stepsRead the figure: switches A and B are in series in a branch connected from the node between the upper R and the LED to ground, so the branch conducts only when A = 1 and B = 1 → Recognise that this closed branch shorts the LED to ground, so the LED (Y) goes OFF when the branch conducts and stays ON otherwise → Build the truth table (00->1, 01->1, 10->1, 11->0) and match it to the gate name among the options
whyThe work is reading a switch-and-LED circuit: spotting that the series switches form a conducting path only when both are closed, then seeing that this path bypasses the LED, which inverts the output, before matching the truth table to a gate.
the catchTrap option: 'AND' is exactly what a candidate gets by stopping at 'two switches in series = AND' and missing that the closed path shorts the LED rather than lighting it; the inversion is shown only by the circuit and is not stated in the stem.

A 800 turn coil of effective area 0.05 m2 is kept perpendicular to a magnetic field 5 × 10-5 T. When the plane of the coil is rotated by 90° around any of its coplanar axis in 0.1 s, the emf induced in the coil will be:
  1. 2 V
  2. 0.2 V
  3. 2 × 10-3 V
  4. 0.02 V

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high

READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A parallel plate capacitor of capacitance 20 μF is being charged by a voltage source whose potential is changing at the rate of 3 V/s. The conduction current through the connecting wires, and the displacement current through the plates of the capacitor, would be, respectively.
  1. Zero, 60 μA

  3. 60 μA, zero
  4. Zero, zero
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Charge on the capacitor is q = CV, so the conduction current in the wires is Ic = dqdt = CdVdt.
  2. Ic = 20×10-6 F × 3 V/s = 60×10-6 A = 60 μA.
  3. Displacement current between the plates is Id = ε0 dΦEdt = ε0 ddt(qε0) = dqdt = Ic.

READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
In an experiment, the percentage of error occurred in the measurement of physical quantities A, B, C and D are 1%, 2%, 3% and 4% respectively. Then the maximum percentage of error in the measurement X, where X = A2B1/2C1/3D3, will be
  1. (313)%
  2. 16%
  3. -10%
  4. 10%

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. For X = A2B1/2C1/3D3, the maximum fractional error is Δ XX = 2Δ AA + 12Δ BB + 13Δ CC + 3Δ DD (errors always add).
  2. Substitute: 2(1%) + 12(2%) + 13(3%) + 3(4%).
  3. = 2% + 1% + 1% + 12% = 16%, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecall the error-propagation rule for X = A^a B^b / (C^c D^d): maximum relative error = a(dA/A) + b(dB/B) + c(dC/C) + d(dD/D), with all terms added regardless of numerator/denominator → Multiply each percentage error by its power: 2x1, 12x2, 13x3, 3x4 → Add the four contributions to get the maximum percentage error
whyThe work is recalling the power-weighted additive rule for relative errors and applying it to four quantities, with fractional powers and one weighted sum.
the catchTwo options (-10% and 10%) are the exact results of the plausible wrong route of subtracting the denominator errors instead of adding them.

Q45Physics
subjectPhysics
chapterMagnetic Effect of Electric Current high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on Ampere's circuital law applied to a current-carrying cylindrical conductor (B grows linearly with d inside, falls as 1/d outside), which is taught in Magnetic Effect of Electric Current.
subtopic 1Ampere's Circuital Law
why this subtopicThe answer (B rising linearly inside to a peak at R, then falling as 1/d outside) follows from applying Ampere's circuital law to a long cylindrical conductor.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A cylindrical conductor of radius R is carrying a constant current. The plot of the magnitude of the magnetic field. B with the distance d from the centre of the conductor, is correctly represented by the figure :
  1. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, its figure spec checked against it (labels and lines)
  2. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, its figure spec checked against it (labels and lines)
  3. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, its figure spec checked against it (labels and lines)
  4. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, its figure spec checked against it (labels and lines)
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Take a circular Amperian loop of radius d coaxial with the conductor, assuming uniform current density.
  2. Inside (d < R): Ienc = Id2R2, so B(2π d) = μ0 I d2R2.
  3. Hence B = μ0 I d2π R2 ∝ d, a straight line from the origin.
  4. Outside (d > R): Ienc = I, so B = μ0 I2π d ∝ 1d.
  5. Both give B = μ0 I2π R at d = R, so the graph peaks continuously at R and decays as 1/d, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsApply Ampere's circuital law outside the conductor (d > R): full current enclosed, so B = μ0 I / (2 π d), falling as 1/d → Apply Ampere's law inside (d < R) with uniform current density: enclosed current I d2/R2, so B = μ0 I d / (2 π R2), rising linearly from zero at the centre → Combine the two pieces (linear rise to a maximum at d = R, then 1/d decay) and match the shape among the four graphs
whyThe work is applying Ampere's law in two regions (inside and outside the cylinder) and combining the resulting linear and 1/d dependences into one graph to pick from four sketches.
the catchThe stem leaves unstated that the conductor is solid with uniformly distributed current; option 2 is exactly the hollow-cylinder (surface current) result and option 1 the result of assuming constant field inside.

Q49Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
chapterStructure of Atom high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on ordering orbital energies by the (n+l) rule used for filling orbitals in atoms, which is taught in Structure of Atom.
subtopic 1Atomic Orbitals: Shapes, Energies and Nodes
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the relative energies of orbitals in a multi-electron atom set by the (n+l) rule (5f=8 > 6p=7 > 5p=6 > 4d=6 with lower n lower energy), which is taught under energies of orbitals.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
4d, 5p, 5f and 6p orbitals are arranged in the order of decreasing energy. The correct option is
  1. 5f > 6p > 5p > 4d
  2. 6p > 5f > 5p > 4d
  3. 6p > 5f > 4d > 5p
  4. 5f > 6p > 4d > 5p
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Apply the (n+l) rule: higher (n+l) means higher energy; for equal (n+l), higher n means higher energy.
  2. 4d: n+l = 4+2 = 6.
  3. 5p: n+l = 5+1 = 6.
  4. 5f: n+l = 5+3 = 8.
  5. 6p: n+l = 6+1 = 7.
  6. 4d and 5p tie at 6; 5p has larger n, so E5p > E4d.
  7. Decreasing energy: 5f > 6p > 5p > 4d, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsCompute (n + l) for each orbital: 4d = 6, 5p = 6, 5f = 8, 6p = 7 → Order by (n + l) to place 5f and 6p above the 4d/5p pair → Break the 4d/5p tie with the rule that the orbital with higher n has higher energy, then match the full sequence to an option
whyRecall of the (n + l) rule from the atomic-structure chapter applied to four orbitals, plus the equal-(n + l) tie-break on one pair, then matching the order to an option.
the catchOptions are paired so that each ordering of 4d/5p appears with each ordering of 5f/6p; a candidate who ranks by n alone (6p above 5f) or who reverses the equal-(n + l) tie-break lands exactly on a distractor.

Q50Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
chapterRedox Reactions high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on recognising disproportionation, where one element in a single oxidation state is both oxidised and reduced, which is a type of redox reaction taught in Redox Reactions.
subtopic 1Types of Redox Reactions
why this subtopicThe answer depends on recognising disproportionation reactions (one species is both oxidised and reduced, as in Cu+ and MnO4^2-), a type of redox reaction.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Which of the following reactions are disproportionation reaction?
Select the correct option from the following
a2Cu+ ⟶ Cu2+ + Cu0
b3MnO42- + 4H+ ⟶ 2MnO4- + MnO2 + 2H2O
c2KMnO4 Δ  K2MnO4 + MnO2 + O2
d2MnO4- + 3Mn2+ + 2H2O ⟶ 5MnO2 + 4H⊕
  1. (a) and (b) only
  2. (a), (b) and (c)
  3. (a), (c) and (d)
  4. (a) and (d) only
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Disproportionation: one element in one oxidation state is oxidised and reduced at the same time.
  2. (a) Cu+ (+1) → Cu2+ (+2) and Cu0 (0): disproportionation.
  3. (b) Mn in MnO42- is +6; it goes to +7 in MnO4- and +4 in MnO2: disproportionation.
  4. (c) Mn in KMnO4 is +7; it goes to +6 (K2MnO4) and +4 (MnO2), so Mn is only reduced; O goes from -2 to 0. Different elements are oxidised and reduced, so this is not disproportionation.
  5. (d) Mn at +7 and +2 both become +4 in MnO2: comproportionation, not disproportionation.
  6. Only (a) and (b) qualify, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsAssign oxidation numbers to Cu and Mn (and O in (c)) on both sides of each of the four reactions → Apply the definition of disproportionation: the same element in one oxidation state is both oxidised and reduced, so check each reaction for one reactant species going both up and down (rejecting the case where two different starting states meet in a middle state, and the case where the element only goes down while a different element is oxidised) → Collect the qualifying reactions and match the set to the four combination options
whyThe work is oxidation-number bookkeeping across four Mn/Cu reactions followed by applying the disproportionation definition to each, then matching the resulting set to an option.
the catchIn (c) Mn ends up in two different oxidation states (+6 and +4), which looks like disproportionation, but both are reductions from +7 and the oxidised element is O; two options include (c). (d) is the reverse (comproportionation) and also appears in options.

why this subtopicThe answer comes from w = -Pext(Vf - Vi) = -2 bar x 0.15 L = -0.3 L bar = -30 J, the pressure-volume work for an isothermal expansion against a constant external pressure (irreversible).

READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Under isothermal condition, a gas at 300 K expands from 0.1 L to 0.25 L against a constant external pressure of 2 bar. The work done by the gas is
(Given that 1 L bar = 100 J)
  1. -30 J
  2. 5 kJ
  3. 25 J
  4. 30 J
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · medium
  1. Change in volume: Δ V = 0.25 L - 0.1 L = 0.15 L.
  2. Work against constant external pressure (NCERT convention): w = -pex Δ V.
  3. w = -(2 bar)(0.15 L) = -0.3 L bar.
  4. -0.3 L bar × 100 J/(L bar) = -30 J, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 mediumagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecognise that expansion against a constant external pressure is irreversible, so w = -p_ext * deltaV applies (not the isothermal reversible nRT ln(V2/V1) form, despite the stated 300 K) → Compute deltaV = 0.25 L - 0.1 L = 0.15 L and w = -2 bar x 0.15 L = -0.3 L bar → Convert with the given factor 1 L bar = 100 J to get the magnitude 30 J and apply the IUPAC sign convention to choose between -30 J and +30 J
whyThe work is one substitution into the chapter's irreversible-work formula plus a unit conversion and a sign-convention decision, after rejecting the reversible isothermal route the stem's temperature invites.
the catchOptions -30 J and +30 J differ only in sign, so the candidate must apply the NCERT sign convention (work done on the system positive) against the stem's wording 'work done by the gas'; the 300 K value is a red herring that tempts the nRT ln route.

READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Which is the correct thermal stability order for H2E (E = O, S, Se, Te and Po)?
  1. H2S < H2O < H2Se < H2Te < H2Po
  2. H2O < H2S < H2Se < H2Te < H2Po
  3. H2Po < H2Te < H2Se < H2S < H2O

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Group 14 tetrahalides are generally covalent, but SnF4 and PbF4 are exceptions that are ionic in nature, so option 4 is correct and option 1 ("PbF4 is covalent") is incorrect.
  2. SiCl4 has vacant d orbitals on Si that accept a lone pair from H2O, so it is easily hydrolysed: SiCl4 + 4H2O → Si(OH)4 + 4HCl; option 2 is correct.
  3. For Ge the inert pair effect is weak, so the +4 state is more stable and GeX4 is more stable than GeX2; option 3 is correct.
  4. Hence the incorrect statement is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecall that SiCl4 hydrolyses readily because Si can accept a lone pair from water into vacant d-orbitals, and check statement 2 against it → Apply the inert pair effect down group 14 to judge whether the +4 state of Ge (GeX4) is more stable than the +2 state (GeX2), and check statement 3 → Recall that SnF4 and PbF4 are ionic (small, highly electronegative F with large Sn4+/Pb4+ cations) rather than covalent like the other tetrahalides, and judge statements 1 and 4 against each other to pick the incorrect one
whyThe candidate must check four separate group-14 halide facts (hydrolysis of SiCl4, inert-pair stability of Ge(IV) vs Ge(II), and the ionic character of SnF4 and PbF4) and pick the one that contradicts them.
the catchStatements 1 and 4 are mutually exclusive in a way that tempts candidates who apply the general rule that tetrahalides are covalent; the ionic nature of SnF4/PbF4 is an exception not stated plainly in the current NCERT text.

Q59Chemistry / inorganic
subjectChemistry
streaminorganic
chapterChemical Bonding and Molecular Structure high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the molecular orbital electronic configuration of C2, whose bond order of 2 comes from two pi bonds alone, which Molecular Orbital Theory in Chemical Bonding teaches.
subtopic 1Molecular Orbital Theory
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the MO electronic configuration of C2, whose four bonding electrons fill only the pi2px and pi2py orbitals and leave no sigma bond, as Molecular Orbital Theory describes.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Which of the following diatomic molecular species has only π bonds according to Molecular Orbital Theory?
  1. O2
  2. N2
  3. C2
  4. Be2
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. C2 has 12 electrons: KK σ 2s2 σ* 2s2 (π 2px2 = π 2py2).
  2. The σ 2s and σ* 2s contributions cancel, and σ 2pz is empty, so bond order = 8-42 = 2 arises entirely from the two π bonds.
  3. N2 and O2 both fill σ 2pz (they contain a σ bond), and Be2 has bond order 0, so the answer is C2, option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict

whyThe candidate has to write MO electronic configurations for four species and see which bonding electrons sit in π rather than σ orbitals, a recall of the MO energy order plus one application per option.

Q60Chemistry / inorganic
subjectChemistry
streaminorganic
chapterClassification of Elements and Periodicity in Properties high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the periodic trend in first ionisation enthalpy across period 2, including the B < Be and O < N anomalies.
subtopic 1Trends in Chemical Properties – Ionization Enthalpy
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the trend in first ionisation enthalpy across period 2, including the Be > B and N > O exceptions.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
For the second period elements the correct increasing order of first ionisation enthalpy is:
  1. Li < Be < B < C < N < O < F < Ne
  2. Li < B < Be < C < O < N < F < Ne
  3. Li < B < Be < C < N < O < F < Ne
  4. Li < Be < B < C < O < N < F < Ne
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Across period 2 the nuclear charge rises and atomic size falls, so first ionisation enthalpy generally increases from Li to Ne.
  2. Be (2s2) loses a penetrating 2s electron while B (2s2 2p1) loses a less penetrating, better shielded 2p electron, so Δi H(B) < Δi H(Be).
  3. N has a stable half-filled 2p3 configuration while O (2p4) loses a paired electron with extra repulsion, so Δi H(O) < Δi H(N).
  4. Hence Li < B < Be < C < O < N < F < Ne, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecall the general trend: first ionisation enthalpy increases across period 2 from Li to Ne with increasing effective nuclear charge → Apply the first anomaly: Be (2s2, removing a penetrating 2s electron) has higher ionisation enthalpy than B (2p1), so B comes before Be → Apply the second anomaly: N (half-filled 2p3) has higher ionisation enthalpy than O (2p4, paired-electron repulsion), so O comes before N, then match the resulting order to an option
whyThe work is one recalled periodic trend plus two separate exceptions (Be/B and N/O) that must both be applied to the eight-element sequence before matching an option.
the catchThe three wrong options are each the exact sequence obtained by applying the plain trend with one or both of the Be/B and N/O exceptions omitted.

chapterIonic Equilibrium high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on converting pH to [OH-] and applying the solubility product expression Ksp = [Ca2+][OH-]^2 for a sparingly soluble salt, which is ionic equilibrium.
subtopic 1Solubility Product Constant
why this subtopicThe answer depends on computing Ksp = s(2s)^2 for Ca(OH)2 from [OH-] = 10^-5 M (pOH 5), giving 0.5 x 10^-15; the pH-to-[OH-] conversion is only an intermediate step.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
pH of a saturated solution of Ca(OH)2 is 9. The solubility product (Ksp) of Ca(OH)2 is:
  1. 0.5 × 10-15
  2. 0.25 × 10-10
  3. 0.125 × 10-15
  4. 0.5 × 10-10
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. pOH = 14 - pH = 14 - 9 = 5.
  2. [OH-] = 10-5 M.
  3. Ca(OH)2 ⇌ Ca2+ + 2OH-, so [Ca2+] = [OH-]2 = 0.5 × 10-5 M.
  4. Ksp = [Ca2+][OH-]2 = (0.5 × 10-5)(10-5)2 = 0.5 × 10-15, which is option 1.

the catchOption 3 is exactly s^3 (dropping the factor 4 in Ksp = 4s^3), and a candidate who sets [Ca2+] equal to [OH-] lands on a different power, so the stoichiometric factor is a trap.

The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

  1. A maximum boiling azeotrope forms when a solution shows large negative deviation from Raoult's law (ptotal < xA pA0 + xB pB0).

chapterChemical Kinetics high confidence

subtopic 1Rate of a Chemical Reaction
why this subtopicThe answer depends on expressing the rate of reaction in terms of each species divided by its stoichiometric coefficient (-d[N2]/dt = 1/2 d[NH3]/dt), which is taught under the rate of a chemical reaction.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
For the chemical reaction
N2(g) + 3H2(g) ⇌ 2NH3(g)
the correct option is:
  1. -13d[H2]dt = -12d[NH3]dt
  2. -d[N2]dt = 2d[NH3]dt
  3. -d[N2]dt = 12d[NH3]dt

The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. For N2 + 3H2 ⇌ 2NH3, divide each rate of change by its stoichiometric coefficient, with a minus sign for reactants.
  2. rate = -d[N2]dt = -13d[H2]dt = +12d[NH3]dt.

subtopic 1Acidity of Phenols
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the oxygen lone pair of phenol being delocalised into the benzene ring by resonance, which makes phenol the hardest to protonate; this effect is taught under acidity of phenols.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The compound that is most difficult to protonate is :
  1. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, every structure checked atom for atom against its SMILES · H2O name ✓
  2. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, every structure checked atom for atom against its SMILES · CH4O name ✓
  3. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, every structure checked atom for atom against its SMILES · C2H6O name ✓

  1. Ease of protonation depends on the availability of the oxygen lone pair (basicity).

Q70Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
chapterSolutions high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the defining property of an ideal solution (zero enthalpy of mixing, obeying Raoult's law), which is taught under ideal and non-ideal solutions in Solutions.
subtopic 1Ideal and Nonideal Solutions
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the defining property of an ideal solution, that the enthalpy of mixing is zero at constant T and P.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
For an ideal solution, the correct option is :
  1. Δmix S = 0 at constant T and P
  2. Δmix V ≠ 0 at constant T and P
  3. Δmix H = 0 at constant T and P
  4. Δmix G = 0 at constant T and P
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. In an ideal solution solute-solvent interactions equal solute-solute and solvent-solvent interactions, so Δmix H = 0 and Δmix V = 0 at constant T and P.
  2. Mixing increases randomness, so Δmix S > 0, ruling out option 1; Δmix V = 0 rules out option 2.
  3. Δmix G = Δmix H - TΔmix S = -TΔmix S < 0, ruling out option 4.
  4. Hence Δmix H = 0 is the correct statement, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultyeasy · 1 step
stepsRecall the NCERT definition of an ideal solution (obeys Raoult's law, with zero enthalpy and zero volume change on mixing) and match it to the options
whyThe work is a single recall of the stated properties of an ideal solution, checked against four mixing-function statements.

READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Conjugate base for Brönsted acids H2O and HF are :
  1. OH- and H2F+, respectively
  2. H3O+ and F-, respectively
  3. OH- and F-, respectively
  4. H3O+ and H2F+, respectively
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. A conjugate base is what remains after a Brönsted acid gives away one proton: HA → A- + H+.
  2. H2O → OH- + H+, so the conjugate base of H2O is OH-.
  3. HF → F- + H+, so the conjugate base of HF is F-, which is option 3.

Q72Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
chapterSurface Chemistry high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the preparation and charge of colloidal sols: the AgI sol is negatively charged when excess I- (excess KI) is adsorbed on the particles, which is taught under colloids in Surface Chemistry.
subtopic 1Charge on Colloidal Particles
why this subtopicThe answer depends on how a sol gets its charge: AgI particles adsorb the common ion present in excess (I- when KI is in excess), giving a negatively charged [AgI]I- sol.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Which mixture of the solutions will lead to the formation of negatively charged colloidal [AgI]I- sol. ?
  1. 50 mL of 1 M AgNO3 + 50 mL of 1.5 M KI
  2. 50 mL of 1 M AgNO3 + 50 mL of 2 M KI
  3. 50 mL of 2 M AgNO3 + 50 mL of 1.5 M KI
  4. 50 mL of 0.1 M AgNO3 + 50 mL of 0.1 M KI
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1 & 2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · low
  1. A negative [AgI]I- sol forms when AgNO3 is added to excess KI, so I- is adsorbed on AgI.
  2. Option 1: n(Ag+) = 0.05 × 1 = 0.05 mol, n(I-) = 0.05 × 1.5 = 0.075 mol, so I- is in excess by 0.025 mol.
  3. Option 2: n(Ag+) = 0.05 mol, n(I-) = 0.05 × 2 = 0.10 mol, so I- is in excess by 0.05 mol.
  4. Option 3: n(Ag+) = 0.10 mol > n(I-) = 0.075 mol, so Ag+ is in excess and the sol would be positive [AgI]Ag+.
  5. Option 4: n(Ag+) = n(I-) = 0.005 mol, no excess ion, so no charged sol is stabilised.
  6. Options 1 and 2 both give excess I-; option 1 has the smaller excess, which favours a stable sol over coagulation by excess electrolyte, so choose option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
1 & 21 lowagree

stepsRecall that an AgI sol is negatively charged ([AgI]I-) when KI is in excess, because the precipitate preferentially adsorbs the common ion I- from the dispersion medium → For each option, compute millimoles of AgNO3 and KI from equal 50 mL volumes (moles proportional to molarity) → Compare the two amounts to find the mixture(s) where I- remains in excess after precipitation, rejecting excess-Ag+ (positive sol) and equimolar mixtures
whyOne recall of the preferential-adsorption rule for charge on AgI sol, then one application: a simple stoichiometric comparison of AgNO3 vs KI amounts across four mixtures.

subtopic 1Chemical Properties of Alkenes

An alkene "A" on reaction with O3 and Zn-H2O gives propanone and ethanal in equimolar ratio. Addition of HCl to alkene "A" gives "B" as the major product. The structure of product "B" is:
  1. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, every structure checked atom for atom against its SMILES · C5H11Cl name ✓
    Cl-CH2-CH2-CH(CH3)2
  2. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, every structure checked atom for atom against its SMILES · C5H11Cl name ✓
    H3C-CH2-CH(CH2Cl)-CH3
  3. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, every structure checked atom for atom against its SMILES · C5H11Cl name ✓
    H3C-CH2-C(CH3)(Cl)-CH3
  4. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, every structure checked atom for atom against its SMILES · C5H11Cl name ✓

no worked solution is printed for this question

  1. Join the two carbonyl carbons of (CH3)2C=O and CH3CHO by a double bond: A = (CH3)2C=CH-CH3 (2-methylbut-2-ene).

Q75Chemistry / inorganic
subjectChemistry
streaminorganic
chapterCoordination Compounds – Practice high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on crystal field splitting of Fe(II) in the strong-field cyanide octahedral complex giving low-spin t2g^6 eg^0, which is crystal field theory in coordination compounds.
subtopic 1Crystal Field Theory and Its Limitations
why this subtopicThe answer t2g^6 eg^0 depends on crystal field splitting of Fe(II) d6 in an octahedral field with strong-field CN- ligands giving a low-spin configuration.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
What is the correct electronic configuration of the central atom in K4[Fe(CN)6] based on crystal field theory?
  1. t2g4 eg2
  2. t2g6 eg0
  3. e3 t23
  4. e4 t22
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Charge balance on [Fe(CN)6]4-: x + 6(-1) = -4 ⇒ x = +2.
  2. Fe2+ has configuration [Ar] 3d6.
  3. Six CN- ligands give an octahedral field, so the split levels are t2g and eg (this rules out options 3 and 4, which are tetrahedral labels).
  4. CN- is a strong-field ligand, so Δo > P and the electrons pair in t2g.
  5. Low spin d6: t2g6 eg0, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsfind the oxidation state of Fe from the charge balance of K4[Fe(CN)6] (Fe2+) and so its d-electron count (3d6) → identify the geometry as octahedral (six CN- ligands), ruling out the tetrahedral e/t2 options → recall that CN- is a strong-field ligand (Δ_o > pairing energy), so the six d electrons pair in t2g as a low-spin configuration
whyThe work is an oxidation-state calculation to get d6, then choosing the octahedral splitting pattern, then applying the strong-field nature of CN- to place all electrons in the lower set.
the catchThe high-spin option t2g4 eg2 is exactly what a candidate gets by forgetting that CN- is a strong-field ligand.

subtopic 1Hybridisation
why this subtopicThe answer depends on PCl5's sp3d trigonal bipyramidal structure, where longer, weaker axial P-Cl bonds make the molecule reactive, as taught under sp3d hybridisation of PCl5.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Identify the incorrect statement related to PCl5 from the following:
  1. Three equatorial P-Cl bonds make an angle of 120° with each other
  2. Two axial P-Cl bonds make an angle of 180° with each other
  3. Axial P-Cl bonds are longer than equatorial P-Cl bonds
  4. PCl5 molecule is non-reactive
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. PCl5 is sp3d hybridised with a trigonal bipyramidal shape: three equatorial P-Cl bonds at 120° and two axial bonds at 180°, so options 1 and 2 are correct.
  2. Each axial bond pair feels more repulsion from the equatorial bond pairs, so axial bonds are longer and weaker than equatorial bonds, so option 3 is correct.
  3. Because the axial bonds are longer and weaker, PCl5 is very reactive, so the statement that it is non-reactive (option 4) is the incorrect one.

Q77Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
chapterIonic Equilibrium high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on recognising that excess weak base NH4OH with its salt NH4Cl (from partial neutralisation by HCl) forms a basic buffer, which is taught under Buffer Solutions in Ionic Equilibrium.
subtopic 1Buffer Solutions
why this subtopicThe answer depends on knowing that excess NH4OH partly neutralised by HCl leaves a weak base with its salt (NH4OH + NH4Cl), which is a basic buffer.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Which will make basic buffer?
  1. 50 mL of 0.1 M NaOH + 25 mL of 0.1 M CH3COOH
  2. 100 mL of 0.1 M CH3COOH + 100 mL of 0.1 M NaOH
  3. 100 mL of 0.1 M HCl + 200 mL of 0.1 M NH4OH
  4. 100 mL of 0.1 M HCl + 100 mL of 0.1 M NaOH
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. A basic buffer needs a weak base together with its salt of a strong acid, e.g. NH4OH + NH4Cl.
  2. Option 3: n(HCl) = 100 × 0.1 = 10 mmol and n(NH4OH) = 200 × 0.1 = 20 mmol.
  3. HCl + NH4OH → NH4Cl + H2O consumes 10 mmol of NH4OH, leaving 10 mmol NH4OH + 10 mmol NH4Cl, a basic buffer.
  4. Option 1: n(NaOH) = 5 mmol > n(CH3COOH) = 2.5 mmol, so excess strong base remains with CH3COONa; not a buffer.
  5. Option 2 gives only CH3COONa (exact neutralisation) and option 4 gives only NaCl; neither is a buffer, so the answer is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecall that a basic buffer is a weak base together with its salt with a strong acid (e.g. NH4OH + NH4Cl), and that strong acid/strong base pairs cannot form one → For each mixture, convert volume x molarity to millimoles and work out what is left after neutralisation (excess reagent and salt formed) → Pick the mixture whose residue is excess weak base plus its conjugate-acid salt, rejecting those that leave excess strong base, only salt, or a weak-acid system
whyThe work is one recall of what a basic buffer is plus a millimole neutralisation count across four mixtures to see which leaves weak base alongside its salt.

Q81Chemistry / inorganic
subjectChemistry
streaminorganic
chapterThe p–Block Elements high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on group 14 halide chemistry: Si can expand to [SiF6]2- with small F atoms but cannot hold six large Cl atoms, so [SiCl6]2- is not stable.
subtopic 1Hydrides, Halides, Oxides, Silicones of Group 14
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the stability of hexahalo complexes of group 14 tetrahalides: [SiF6]2- forms but [SiCl6]2- does not, because six large Cl atoms cannot fit around small Si and Cl lone pairs interact poorly with Si, which is taught under the halides of group 14.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Which of the following species is not stable?
  1. [SiF6]2-
  2. [GeCl6]2-
  3. [Sn(OH)6]2-
  4. [SiCl6]2-
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Group 14 elements beyond carbon can expand coordination to six using d orbitals, forming [MX6]2- species.
  2. Si is small and F is small, so six F fit around Si: [SiF6]2- is stable.
  3. Ge and Sn are larger, so [GeCl6]2- and [Sn(OH)6]2- are stable.
  4. Six large Cl atoms cannot be accommodated around small Si (steric repulsion, weak lone-pair interaction), so [SiCl6]2- is not stable, which is option 4.

whyThe work is one NCERT recall (group 14 hexa-coordinate species via d-orbital availability) plus one application of a central-atom-size versus ligand-size steric comparison across four species.

  1. Z = pVmRT = Vm,realVm,ideal.
  2. Molar volume is 20% smaller, so Vm,real = 0.8 Vm,ideal and Z = 0.8 < 1.
  3. Z < 1 means the gas is more compressible than an ideal gas, so attractive forces are dominant, which is option 3.

READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A compound is formed by cation C and anion A. The anions form hexagonal close packed (hcp) lattice and the cations occupy 75% of octahedral voids. The formula of the compound is :
  1. C2A3
  2. C3A2
  3. C3A4
  4. C4A3
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. In an hcp lattice of N anions there are N octahedral voids.
  2. Cations occupy 75% of them: number of C = 0.75N = 34N.
  3. C:A = 34N : N = 3:4.
  4. The formula is C3A4, which is option 3.

Q86Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
chapterThermodynamics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on entropy decreasing when gaseous moles decrease (2H(g) -> H2(g)), which is the entropy concept taught in Thermodynamics.
subtopic 1Entropy, Second Law & Third Law
why this subtopicThe answer depends on entropy decreasing when two gaseous H atoms combine into one H2 molecule (fewer gas particles, less disorder), which is taught under entropy.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
In which case change in entropy is negative?
  1. Evaporation of water
  2. Expansion of a gas at constant temperature
  3. Sublimation of solid to gas
  4. 2H(g) → H2(g)
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Evaporation (liquid → gas) increases disorder, so Δ S > 0.
  2. Isothermal expansion of a gas increases volume: Δ S = nRlnV2V1 > 0.
  3. Sublimation (solid → gas) increases disorder, so Δ S > 0.
  4. For 2H(g) → H2(g), Δ ng = 1 - 2 = -1, so the system becomes more ordered and Δ S < 0, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultyeasy · 1 step
stepsRecall that entropy falls when disorder or number of gaseous particles decreases, and pick the option where gas particles combine (fewer moles of gas) rather than the phase changes to gas or isothermal expansion that raise entropy
whyA single recall of the qualitative rule linking entropy change to phase change, volume and number of gaseous moles, applied by inspection to four standard textbook examples.

Q90Chemistry / organic
subjectChemistry
streamorganic
chapterAmines high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the order of basic strength of methyl substituted amines in water, set by inductive effect, solvation and steric hindrance, which the Amines chapter teaches under basicity.
subtopic 1Chemical Reactions of Amines (Basicity, Alkylation and Acylation)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the order of basic strength of methyl-substituted amines in water (secondary > primary > tertiary, from inductive effect, solvation and steric factors), which is taught under the basicity of amines.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The correct order of the basic strength of methyl substituted amines in aqueous solution is :
  1. (CH3)2NH > CH3NH2 > (CH3)3N
  2. (CH3)3N > CH3NH2 > (CH3)2NH
  3. (CH3)3N > (CH3)2NH > CH3NH2
  4. CH3NH2 > (CH3)2NH > (CH3)3N
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Inductive (+I) effect alone would give (CH3)3N > (CH3)2NH > CH3NH2, as seen in the gas phase.
  2. In aqueous solution the protonated amine is stabilised by H-bonding with water, which is greatest for CH3NH3+ (three N-H) and least for (CH3)3NH+ (one N-H); steric hindrance to solvation also rises with substitution.
  3. Combining these effects for methyl groups gives (CH3)2NH > CH3NH2 > (CH3)3N, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultyeasy · 1 step
stepsRecall the NCERT-stated order of basic strength of methyl-substituted amines in aqueous solution (which balances inductive effect, solvation by hydrogen bonding and steric hindrance) and match it to the option
whyThe work is a single recall of the aqueous-phase basicity order of methylamine, dimethylamine and trimethylamine that the Amines chapter states explicitly, then matching it to one option.
the catchOne option is the gas-phase order (inductive effect alone), a ready distractor for a candidate who reasons from +I effect without accounting for solvation and steric effects in water.

READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider following features

Select the correct option of animal groups which possess all the above characteristics
(a)Organ system level of organisation
(b)Bilateral symmetry
(c)True coelomates with segmentation of body
  1. Annelida, Arthropoda and Chordata
  2. Annelida, Arthropoda and Mollusca
  3. Arthropoda, Mollusca and Chordata
  4. Annelida, Mollusca and Chordata
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. All three features (organ system level, bilateral symmetry, true coelom with segmentation) must hold for each group named in the option.
  2. Annelida: organ system level, bilateral, coelomate, metamerically segmented, so it qualifies.
  3. Arthropoda: organ system level, bilateral, coelomate, segmented body, so it qualifies.
  4. Chordata: organ system level, bilateral, coelomate, segmented (e.g. myotomes, vertebral column), so it qualifies.
  5. Mollusca: organ system level, bilateral, coelomate but unsegmented, so it fails feature (c).
  6. Options 2, 3 and 4 each include Mollusca, so the answer is option 1 (Annelida, Arthropoda and Chordata).

Select the correct sequence of organs in the alimentary canal of cockroach starting from mouth
  1. Pharynx → Oesophagus → Crop → Gizzard → Ileum → Colon → Rectum
  2. Pharynx → Oesophagus → Gizzard → Crop → Ileum → Colon → Rectum
  3. Pharynx → Oesophagus → Gizzard → Ileum → Crop → Colon → Rectum

The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. The mouth opens into the pharynx, which leads to the oesophagus; the oesophagus widens into the sac-like crop, which is followed by the gizzard (proventriculus), then the midgut, and the hindgut runs ileum → colon → rectum. This is option 1.

Q105Biology / zoology
subjectBiology
branchzoology
chapterBody Fluids and Circulation high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on which ECG wave stands for which event: the P-wave is atrial depolarisation, the QRS complex is ventricular depolarisation, the T-wave is ventricular repolarisation, and a smaller T-wave points to coronary ischemia. The ECG is taught in Body Fluids and Circulation.
subtopic 1Electrocardiogram (ECG) and Double Circulation
why this subtopicThe answer depends on what the ECG waves represent (P-wave atrial depolarisation, QRS ventricular depolarisation, T-wave ventricular repolarisation, reduced T-wave in coronary ischemia), which is taught under the electrocardiogram.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Match the Column-I with Column-II
Select the correct option.
aP - waveiDepolarisation of ventricles
bQRS complexiiRepolarisation of ventricles
cT - waveiiiCoronary ischemia
dReduction in the size of T-waveivDepolarisation of atira
vRepolarisation of atria
  1. (a) (iv), (b) (i), (c) (ii), (d) (iii)
  2. (a) (iv), (b) (i), (c) (ii), (d) (v)
  3. (a) (ii), (b) (i), (c) (v), (d) (iii)
  4. (a) (ii), (b) (iii), (c) (v), (d) (iv)
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. The P-wave represents electrical excitation (depolarisation) of the atria, so a → iv.
  2. The QRS complex represents depolarisation of the ventricles, so b → i.
  3. The T-wave represents the return of the ventricles from excited to normal state (repolarisation), so c → ii.
  4. A reduction in the size of the T-wave indicates insufficient oxygen supply to heart muscle, i.e. coronary ischemia, so d → iii.
  5. The match a-iv, b-i, c-ii, d-iii is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRecall the standard ECG wave meanings (P = atrial depolarisation, QRS = ventricular depolarisation, T = ventricular repolarisation); fixing (a) eliminates two of the four options → Recall the clinical significance of a reduced T-wave (coronary ischemia/coronary artery disease) to decide (d) between the two remaining options, which differ only in that entry
whyThe work is two linked recalls from the NCERT ECG section: the wave-to-event mapping that cuts the options to two, then the clinical meaning of a shrunken T-wave that separates those two.

Q107Biology / zoology
subjectBiology
branchzoology
chapterEvolution high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on recognising stabilizing selection: the intermediate weight is favoured and both extremes are eliminated. Natural selection is taught in Evolution.
subtopic 1Types of Natural Selection
why this subtopicThe answer depends on recognising stabilizing selection (survival of mean-weight individuals, elimination of both extremes), which is one of the types of natural selection.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
In a species, the weight of newborn ranges from 2 to 5 kg. 97% of the newborn with an average weight between 3 to 3.3 kg survive whereas 99% of the infants born with weight from 2 to 2.5 kg or 4.5 to 5 kg die. Which type of selection process is taking place?
  1. Directional Selection
  2. Stabilizing Selection
  3. Disruptive Selection
  4. Cyclical Selection
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Newborns of intermediate weight (3 to 3.3 kg) survive at 97% while both extremes (2 to 2.5 kg and 4.5 to 5 kg) die at 99%; selection against both tails favouring the mean is stabilizing selection, option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRead the survival data: intermediate-weight newborns (3-3.3 kg) survive while both extremes (2-2.5 kg and 4.5-5 kg) are eliminated → Map 'mean value favoured, both tails selected against' to the NCERT definition of stabilizing selection and reject directional (one tail favoured) and disruptive (both tails favoured)
whyThe candidate must interpret a numerical survival pattern as selection against both extremes and then apply the recalled NCERT classification of natural selection types to it.
